		<item>
		<title>Work begins on Jackson-Milton field house</title>
		<link>https://www.vindy.com/news/local-news/2026/10/work-begins-on-jackson-milton-field-house/</link>
		
		<dc:creator><![CDATA[Rick Logan]]></dc:creator>
		<pubDate>Mon, 26 Oct 2026 04:00:57 +0000</pubDate>
				<category><![CDATA[Local News]]></category>
		<guid isPermaLink="false">https://www.vindy.com/?p=674280</guid>

					<description><![CDATA[NORTH JACKSON —— The Jackson-Milton Local School District has broken ground for its new expansive field house to support the school system&#8217;s various sports teams. “The field house is approximately 30,000 square feet. We’re going to house our wrestling here … currently they practice down at the old stadium, but everything will be on campus [&#8230;]]]></description>
										<content:encoded><![CDATA[<p>NORTH JACKSON —— The Jackson-Milton Local School District has broken ground for its new expansive field house to support the school system&#8217;s various sports teams.</p>
<p>“The field house is approximately 30,000 square feet. We’re going to house our wrestling here … currently they practice down at the old stadium, but everything will be on campus </p>
<p>now,” said Kirk Baker, superintendent of Jackson-Milton schools.</p>
<p>“Down below, it’s going to have a 40-yard indoor turf for softball and baseball practice … We’re going to have locker rooms for the visitor [teams] and home team for us, and then we’re going to have restrooms for everybody,&#8221; he added.</p>
<p>Concession stands, office and storage space also are planned for the field house.</p>
<p>Baker said he considers the new field house to be a major upgrade to existing facilities for student athletes and visitors to the facility.</p>
<p>“Right now, currently at our football field, we’re using porta potties right now unless you have to go in the school for something,” Baker said. “Right now our concession stands are in a shed that we bought, so it’s going to be a major upgrade,&#8221; he said.</p>
<p>Planning for the field house began years ago when the school’s administration saw a need for new facilities after the district&#8217;s stadium was moved to Jackson-Milton’s main campus.</p>
<p>“We’ve been planning to build this field house for probably seven to eight years. It was probably four years ago we knew that we either had to put money into the old stadium because the bleachers needed all redone, and the parking lot was kind of a mess,” Baker said. </p>
<p>As for why it’s taken years, Baker explained that the school’s administration needed that time to gather funding for the project. He praised the Jackson-Milton Board of Education for saving the funds needed for the field house.</p>
<p>“[It’s] just taken us a long time to save up the money,” Baker said. “The board has done a great job in being proactive and saving money to build this, so we have the money to build it now.”</p>
<p>The field house was designed by BSHM Architects and will be built by Brock Construction Co. Baker gave a tentative timeline for the field house’s progress, which is dependent on weather conditions for this upcoming winter.</p>
<p>“We did the ground breaking, but they’re supposed to break ground in two weeks. They’re going to start with the sewer lines and the water lines, and run them back. Then they’re going to start working on the foundation, and the metal and everything has been ordered. That’s supposed to be delivered in January or February,” Baker said.</p>
<p>He projects completion by September depending on weather conditions.</p>
<p>“It’s a great time to be a Blue Jay, and I think it’s going to be a very exciting time when it opens,” Baker said. “It’s going to promote a lot of good, positive feedback I think once the community sees it up and running, and all the kids I think are going to enjoy it.”</p>
]]></content:encoded>

		<item>
		<title>Hubbard Army veteran fought, taught over 3 decades</title>
		<link>https://www.vindy.com/uncategorized/2026/10/hubbard-army-veteran-fought-taught-over-3-decades/</link>
		
		<dc:creator><![CDATA[Rick Logan]]></dc:creator>
		<pubDate>Mon, 26 Oct 2026 04:00:48 +0000</pubDate>
				<category><![CDATA[Local News]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[Uncategorized]]></category>
		<guid isPermaLink="false">https://www.vindy.com/?p=674306</guid>

					<description><![CDATA[For more than three decades, Brent Herzberger answered the call to serve, building a military career that took him from the deserts of Kuwait and Iraq to leadership positions training soldiers and supporting military operations across Ohio. Along the way, the Brookfield High School graduate gained lifelong friendships, valuable lessons and a deep appreciation for [&#8230;]]]></description>
										<content:encoded><![CDATA[<p>For more than three decades, Brent Herzberger answered the call to serve, building a military career that took him from the deserts of Kuwait and Iraq to leadership positions training soldiers and supporting military operations across Ohio. </p>
<p>Along the way, the Brookfield High School graduate gained lifelong friendships, valuable lessons and a deep appreciation for those who have worn the nation&#8217;s uniform.</p>
<p>Born in Sharon, Pa., and raised in Masury, Herzberger graduated from Brookfield High School in 1989. The oldest of three children and the only son, he spent much of his youth outdoors, participating in Boy Scouts and exploring the woods with friends.</p>
<p>“I spent a lot of summers playing Army,” Herzberger recalled. “As I entered high school, my mind was definitely focused toward military service.”</p>
<p>That desire to serve was shaped by family members who served in previous wars. His grandfathers were World War II veterans, and an uncle retired from the U.S. Air Force. Listening to their stories instilled a strong sense of patriotism and duty.</p>
<p>After moving to Florida following graduation, Herzberger voluntarily enlisted in the Army in December 1989.</p>
<p>His military career began as a combat engineer and ribbon bridge crew member before transitioning into construction engineering specialties. As he advanced through the ranks, his responsibilities grew. He helped establish three new engineer units under the 112th Engineer Battalion at Camp James A. Garfield and later served as a Regional Training Institute course manager, overseeing leadership training for newly promoted sergeants. His final assignment was as a Range Operations noncommissioned officer at Camp James A. Garfield, where he managed personnel and training operations at the 21,000-acre military training center.</p>
<p>Like many veterans, he remembers basic training as an adjustment.</p>
<p>“It was a culture shock going from your schedule to their schedule,” he said. “But eventually you got into a rhythm.”</p>
<p>His first deployment came during Operation Desert Storm in 1990. While there were long periods of waiting before the ground war began, life quickly changed once operations started.</p>
<p>“We were living out of our vehicles, always on the move,” Herzberger said. “No tents, latrines or mess halls.”</p>
<p>His second deployment, with Charlie Company, 216th Engineer Battalion of Austintown during Operation Iraqi Freedom in 2004, was markedly different. Engineers conducted hundreds of combat patrols, logged thousands of equipment hours and completed major force-protection projects to support troops throughout Iraq.</p>
<p>One of the lasting effects of deployment, he said, is the heightened awareness many veterans carry home with them.</p>
<p>“You find yourself scanning the road when traveling, identifying exits when you enter a room and observing everything and everyone coming into your space,” he said. “Stay alert to stay alive.”</p>
<p>Among his most vivid memories are the soldiers his battalion lost during the 2004-05 deployment.</p>
<p>“They will never be forgotten,” he said.</p>
<p>Despite the hardships, Herzberger says the friendships formed during military service remain one of the greatest rewards of his career.</p>
<p>“These are some of the best people I&#8217;ve had the honor of serving with,” he said. “The military family is real.”</p>
<p>Looking back, he credits the Army with giving him discipline, purpose and direction. During his years of service, he earned a bachelor&#8217;s degree and graduated summa cum laude. He also credits the military mindset with helping him raise successful children and achieve personal and professional goals.</p>
<p>After serving 33½ years — 10 in the National Guard and the remainder on active duty — Herzberger retired from the Army in June 2023. Military service continues to be a family tradition; his son is currently serving in the Air Force.</p>
<p>Today, he remains active in the veteran community as senior vice commander of Hubbard VFW Post 3767, helping fellow veterans connect with resources and maintain the bonds forged through military service.</p>
<p>Retirement has also allowed him more time with family and a new passion: participating in a World War II living history and reenactment group.</p>
<p>“We can&#8217;t let that piece of history fade away,” he said. “We must become the stewards of their stories and keep their service and sacrifices alive.”</p>
<p>For Herzberger, preserving the legacy of those who served before him is simply another way of continuing a lifetime of service.</p>
]]></content:encoded>

		<guid isPermaLink="false">https://www.vindy.com/?p=674346</guid>

					<description><![CDATA[LOWELLVILLE —— Celebrating its past and present while working toward an even brighter future, Inspiring Minds Youngstown hosted its annual fundraiser, Black &#038; White Ball, on Saturday at the Community Literacy Workforce and Cultural Center. Since 2015, IM Youngstown has provided after-school and summer enrichment programming to nearly 1,000 Youngstown grade school and high school [&#8230;]]]></description>
										<content:encoded><![CDATA[<p>LOWELLVILLE —— Celebrating its past and present while working toward an even brighter future, Inspiring Minds Youngstown hosted its annual fundraiser, Black &#038; White Ball, on Saturday at the Community Literacy Workforce and Cultural Center.</p>
<p>     Since 2015, IM Youngstown has provided after-school and summer enrichment programming to nearly 1,000 Youngstown grade school and high school students. Its “scholars” (aka students) have achieved a 100% graduation rate among those who continued their education at college, joined the armed forces or entered the workforce.</p>
<p>      Its stated mission at imyouth.org aims “to engage, inspire and empower youth to reach their full potential through education and exposure to life-changing experiences.”</p>
<p>IM Youngstown scholars have visited more than 30 cities in 15 states and four countries, toured more than 50 colleges and universities and have been exposed to more than 40 companies and industries.</p>
<p>A freshman at Ursuline High School, Mya Young, excitedly spoke of the IMY trip to Baltimore last summer that included stops at nearby colleges.  </p>
<p>She has spent two years with IM Youngstown and, with IMY guiding her, has read her poetry in public and co-hosts a radio show. The experience and influence has made her work towards her goal of graduating high school and attending a university on the path to become a doctor.</p>
<p>    The evening featured the usual elements such events —— dinner, basket and 50/50 raffle and deejay &#8212; but, more importantly, it included speeches and testimonials from those involved with the involved with non-profit organization.</p>
<p>      Referring to the event’s theme of “Future Impact Stars Here,” Mahoning Valley Board Member Angela Moss said, “As we look forward to the future, my hope is that we continue building something that doesn&#8217;t just serve scholars today, but it creates a lasting impact for generations to come because when we invest in our children, we&#8217;re not just changing the future, we&#8217;re helping change the future of our entire community.&#8221;</p>
<p>    She reminded those in attendance, who later were encouraged to donate in the envelopes provided on each table, what it takes to help the next generation be successful.</p>
<p>   &#8220;I also believe it takes a village to build the kind of community where every child has the opportunity to succeed. That Village includes parents. It includes teachers and mentors. It includes businesses and community leaders and supporters, just like you who are sitting here tonight.</p>
<p>“Each of us has something that we can contribute and when it comes together, we can create opportunities that none of us could create alone.&#8221;</p>
<p>   Speaking on the importance of having IMY for the community, Youngstown Mayor Derrick McDowell spoke of his background and that “inspiration was not to be found for me.” He then stated several times that he was not seeking sympathy, just presenting the facts of his upbringing.</p>
<p>He then pointed out, “Right here, right now is the acknowledgment that what you have goes well beyond what many in this room probably would have got. So, what we give to you is a responsibility that to whom much is given, much is required of you, and you are already meeting that expectation.</p>
<p>“I want to tell you that you are our future. You are our present as well. We are relying on you. We expect a wonderful gift that which you go on and give away what you have been graciously given.”</p>
<p>     Superintendent of the Youngstown City Schools Jeremy J. Batchelor provided a filmed speech to the gathering. Another video offered a brief history of IM Youngstown and gave a tour of its new, larger facility at 2110 South Ave., Youngstown. Provided there are classrooms and tutoring rooms, study lofts, a training kitchen with a dining cafe and recreation and multipurpose areas.</p>
<p>     Besides raising funds for IM Youngstown, its scholars worked together to donate money to an area non-profit. Because October is Breast Cancer Awareness Month, this year’s recipient went to Surviving Sisters United.</p>
<p>     IMY Program Coordinator Tammy Hardin explained how this came about. “We are trying to teach our kids how to be leaders, but not only be leaders in the community, but also be able to give back in the community. So, their first order of business as a youth board was to find an organization in the area.</p>
<p>“Surviving Sisters were chosen because we are doing the award in honor of our board member, Trinette Simon, who is also a breast cancer survivor and been with Inspiring Minds for over 10 years. Surviving Sisters United is founded by Cherie Eldridge, who is also a breast cancer survivor. She&#8217;s been connected with Inspiring Minds as well. So, the kids chose her as a nonprofit organization to do a community investment.”  </p>
]]></content:encoded>

		<title>Valley Hispanic group marks 54 years of service</title>
		<link>https://www.vindy.com/news/local-news/2026/10/valley-hispanic-group-marks-54-years-of-service/</link>
		
		<dc:creator><![CDATA[Rick Logan]]></dc:creator>
		<pubDate>Mon, 26 Oct 2026 04:00:29 +0000</pubDate>
				<category><![CDATA[Latest News]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[Local News]]></category>
		<guid isPermaLink="false">https://www.vindy.com/?p=674355</guid>

					<description><![CDATA[BOARDMAN —— Christina Olivera fondly recalled playing with children around her age as her mother stood in line at the Organizacion Civica y Cultural Hispana Americana organization&#8217;s location on Youngstown&#8217;s East Side to receive a block of government-provided cheese. &#8220;It&#8217;s a serious pillar in the Spanish community for resources,&#8221; Olivera, 49, of Youngstown, said, referring [&#8230;]]]></description>
										<content:encoded><![CDATA[<p>BOARDMAN —— Christina Olivera fondly recalled playing with children around her age as her mother stood in line at the Organizacion Civica y Cultural Hispana Americana organization&#8217;s location on Youngstown&#8217;s East Side to receive a block of government-provided cheese.</p>
<p>&#8220;It&#8217;s a serious pillar in the Spanish community for resources,&#8221; Olivera, 49, of Youngstown, said, referring to OCCHA, which, in the 1980s, operated in a small building on Fruit Street.</p>
<p>Four decades later, though, large was more the operative word to describe the organization&#8217;s 54th annual fundraiser gala Saturday evening at Mr. Anthony&#8217;s Event Center, 7440 South Ave. </p>
<p>Olivera and her close friend, Kelli Zurko, both of Youngstown, were among the estimated 300 to 350 elected officials, community leaders and others who attended the elegant and formal gathering, which is the organization&#8217;s largest annual fundraiser.</p>
<p>More than providing cheese for meals, OCCHA gave Olivera, who is a home healthcare aide, many opportunities to boost her self-esteem and self-confidence. Being part of the organization as a child also allowed her to make friends as well as recognize and better appreciate differences in other children, she recalled.</p>
<p>&#8220;OCCHA has always been there for everybody,&#8221; Olivera said.</p>
<p>In addition, the organization allowed her to develop and cultivate a strong bond with Kelli Zurko, 49, of Youngstown, who also works as a home healthcare aide. Both women remain close friends.</p>
<p>Engaging in sports such as kickball, volleyball and jump rope was among Zurko&#8217;s favorite OCCHA-related activities, along with attending yearly summer camps, she remembered.</p>
<p>Perhaps the greatest takeaway from her childhood associations with the organization was how it helped her family, some of whom came from Puerto Rico, improve their English, Zurko said, adding that she&#8217;s grateful that OCCHA also offers scholarships and gives back to the community. </p>
<p>OCCHA was established in March 1972 to serve the Hispanic community and today, the organization holds steadfast to its mission of providing the community with the resources it needs, Angelica Diaz, executive director, said in her remarks Saturday. </p>
<p>To that end, OCCHA has increased the number of annual job fairs it hosts from one to three. It also has partnered with various statewide initiatives and has further highlighted its youth programs, she noted.</p>
<p>More than 70 children in kindergarten through grade six were serviced through this summer&#8217;s youth program, Diaz said, adding that OCCHA has partnered with the Youngstown City Schools and Students Motivated by the Arts (SMARTS). In addition, the organization, which serves about 1,000 families annually, has worked to connect young people with the Youngstown Police Department to instill trust between the youth and police officers, Diaz said. She added that a goal of the gala was to raise at least $50,000 for OCCHA&#8217;s programs and operations.</p>
<p>Also, the organization distributed 19 scholarships for more than $15,000 to graduate and nontraditional students, she continued.</p>
<p>&#8220;We envision opportunity and growth,&#8221; Diaz said, adding that OCCHA places a high priority on investing in young people.</p>
<p>During her presentation, Diaz also pointed out that her organization seeks to serve anyone who needs resources, not just the Hispanic community. </p>
<p>OCCHA&#8217;s programs and services include food and clothing assistance, social service programs, information and referrals, technical assistance, community-resource navigation, workforce-development efforts, employment retreats, job fairs, mental and behavioral health programs, interpretation support, health, youth and scholarship programs, English and computer classes, financial-literacy workshops, home education courses and a yearly toy giveaway. </p>
<p>Diaz expressed gratitude toward those who continue to support OCCHA, including new supporters, as well as pride that more than a half century later, the organization is able to continue its work in the community. </p>
]]></content:encoded>
					
		
		
			</item>

		<title>Obama rallies for Acton, Brown but blasts Trump</title>
		<link>https://www.vindy.com/uncategorized/2026/10/obama-rallies-for-acton-brown-but-blasts-trump/</link>
		
		<dc:creator><![CDATA[Rick Logan]]></dc:creator>
		<pubDate>Mon, 26 Oct 2026 04:00:24 +0000</pubDate>
				<category><![CDATA[Latest News]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[Local News]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[Uncategorized]]></category>
		<guid isPermaLink="false">https://www.vindy.com/?p=674284</guid>

					<description><![CDATA[CLEVELAND — Former President Barack Obama seized on what he described as cruelty and indecency coming from President Donald Trump and his Republican allies, stepping into one of the nation’s ugliest midterm fights as he worked to rally Democratic voters in battleground Ohio on Saturday. Obama took specific aim at Republican gubernatorial candidate Vivek Ramaswamy, [&#8230;]]]></description>
										<content:encoded><![CDATA[
<p>CLEVELAND — Former President Barack Obama seized on what he described as cruelty and indecency coming from President Donald Trump and his Republican allies, stepping into one of the nation’s ugliest midterm fights as he worked to rally Democratic voters in battleground Ohio on Saturday.</p>
<p>Obama took specific aim at Republican gubernatorial candidate Vivek Ramaswamy, who has doubled down on an advertising campaign featuring the estranged mother of Democratic rival Dr. Amy Acton. Trump and Vice President JD Vance have also defended the ad, even as Republican leaders across Ohio, including Gov. Mike DeWine, have called for its removal.</p>
<p>&#8220;In recent days, we&#8217;ve seen there is no line he will not cross, no low he will not sink to,&#8221; Obama said of Ramaswamy, noting that Acton has &#8220;spoken movingly about her painful childhood and all she&#8217;s experienced: abuse, neglect and homelessness.&#8221;</p>
<p>&#8220;There&#8217;s a decency we should expect out of our public officials,&#8221; Obama continued. &#8220;And I don&#8217;t know what&#8217;s happened where the standards of behavior are so low that somebody could run an ad like that.&#8221;</p>
<p>Obama, 65, led the rally at Cleveland Public Auditorium before an estimated crowd of about 10,000.</p>
<p> He remains the Democratic Party&#8217;s best motivator nearly a decade after leaving the White House. He later campaigned Saturday night in Michigan for Democrats in that state</p>
<p>In Cleveland, Obama shared the stage with Acton and former three-term Ohio Sen. Sherrod Brown, who is looking to unseat Republican Sen. Jon Husted.</p>
<p>Although Ohio has become more reliably Republican, it’s been a hotly contested battleground this year. Trump and Vance have both visited the state in recent weeks to drum up support for Husted and Ramaswamy. And the controversy over Ramaswamy’s ad has ratcheted up the already high temperature.</p>
<p>Ramaswamy has defended his decision to press ahead with the ad as fair game. Trump aggressively defended the ad, which has emerged as a national flashpoint in the midterms’ final days, as he voted in West Palm Beach on Saturday.</p>
<p>&#8220;This is a disgrace,” Trump said. &#8220;The mother said that Amy Acton is a liar and she&#8217;s no good. And you know what? I don&#8217;t think you can get a better reference on that.&#8221; He added that if his own mother had said anything bad about him, it would be front and center.</p>
<p>The Ramaswamy campaign video features Acton&#8217;s birth mother, Donna Arthur, calling her &#8220;a liar&#8221; and insinuating she has mental health issues.</p>
<p>Acton, Ohio&#8217;s COVID-19-era health director, has made no secret of facing homelessness and abuse as a child. Arthur lost custody of her children after their stepfather, Arthur&#8217;s husband, was indicted on a charge of rape involving Acton.</p>
<p>The abuse allegedly happened from December 1976 to April 1978. Cledith Arthur failed to show for a court appearance and became a fugitive. Donna Arthur remained married to him until his death in 2004.</p>
<p>Appearing before Obama, Acton spoke as if addressing Ramaswamy directly.</p>
<p>&#8220;My opponent has chosen to exploit, intentionally, my childhood to save his stumbling campaign, but it&#8217;s not going to work,” she said. &#8220;Decency still matters.&#8221; She added: &#8220;Mr. Ramaswamy, you are not just out of touch, you are out for yourself and you are a danger to my state. Apparently there is no low to which you won&#8217;t stoop.&#8221;</p>
<p>After wrapping up in Ohio, Obama led a rally in Detroit for Abdul El-Sayed, a former public health official who is working to unite the Democratic base in Michigan after winning a divisive primary by less than a percentage point. El-Sayed is running against former Rep. Mike Rogers, a Republican.</p>
<p>Obama&#8217;s appearance in the city, which is three-quarters Black, could bolster support for El-Sayed among Black voters. In the primary, his opponent Haley Stevens won Wayne County, which includes Detroit, by about 6 percentage points. Former Vice President Kamala Harris, the first black and South Asian vice president, campaigned for El-Sayed in September.</p>
<p>In Ohio, Obama also called out Trump’s inability to lower costs, a key campaign promise, noting that the president has instead focused on building a new White House ballroom and helipad on the South Lawn.</p>
<p>&#8220;I don&#8217;t know if you&#8217;ve noticed, but he seems very focused on home renovation,” he quipped. &#8220;He should be on HGTV.&#8221; He went on: &#8220;There’s gold everywhere. He&#8217;s got the White House looking like Saddam Hussein opened a Cheesecake Factory.&#8221;</p>
<p>The former Democratic president acknowledged that the nation has Trump for another two years, until his term ends. But voters are not stuck with Trump&#8217;s allies, he said.</p>
<p>&#8220;We&#8217;re not stuck with a spineless Congress and Republican governors who are handling lawlessness and carelessness and cruelty and indifference for working people,&#8221; Obama said. &#8220;After two years of chaos and cruelty and corruption and incompetence &#8230; you get a chance to say enough is enough.&#8221;</p>
<p>Brown urged the crowd to seek change in their Senate leadership.</p>
<p>“Ohioans won&#8217;t stand for this rigged system any longer,” Brown said. “Ohio&#8217;s fighting back.”</p>
<p>The Husted campaign released a statement after Brown’s appearance with Obama.</p>
<p>“The sight of Sherrod Brown and Barack Obama together again should send shivers down Ohioans’ spines,” it read. “The Brown-Obama duo gave us higher taxes, less jobs and wide-open borders. Ohioans are ready to retire Sherrod Brown once and for all by electing Jon Husted on Nov. 3.”</p>
]]></content:encoded>

		<title>Nominations sought for WRTA nonprofit awards</title>
		<link>https://www.vindy.com/uncategorized/2026/10/nominations-sought-for-wrta-nonprofit-awards/</link>
		
		<dc:creator><![CDATA[Bob Coupland]]></dc:creator>
		<pubDate>Mon, 26 Oct 2026 04:00:10 +0000</pubDate>
				<category><![CDATA[Latest News]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[Local News]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[Uncategorized]]></category>
		<guid isPermaLink="false">https://www.vindy.com/?p=674298</guid>

					<description><![CDATA[YOUNGSTOWN —— The Western Reserve Transit Authority is accepting nominations for its 2026 WRTA Nonprofit Awards. Six local nonprofits will be honored at a dinner on Nov. 11 at the B&#038;O Station Banquet Hall, 530 Mahoning Ave., Youngstown. Dean Harris, CEO of WRTA, said each winner receives a WRTA Nonprofit Award, recognition at a special [&#8230;]]]></description>
										<content:encoded><![CDATA[<p>YOUNGSTOWN —— The Western Reserve Transit Authority is accepting nominations for its 2026 WRTA Nonprofit Awards. </p>
<p>Six local nonprofits will be honored at a dinner on Nov. 11 at the B&#038;O Station Banquet Hall, 530 Mahoning Ave., Youngstown.</p>
<p>Dean Harris, CEO of WRTA, said each winner receives a WRTA Nonprofit Award, recognition at a special dinner and a shared regional marketing campaign to help further its mission. One of the six winners will receive an additional honor and be named WRTA 2026 Nonprofit of the Year at the dinner and also receive a $1,000 donation.</p>
<p>Since its inception, 50 Mahoning Valley nonprofits have been recognized. Past nominees and past winners are eligible again after two years.</p>
<p>“Local non-profit organizations do the work most people never see, and the Valley depends on them. Many of their clients, their volunteers, and their staff ride WRTA. So, we try go the extra mile and be a little innovative. Instead of just honoring them at a dinner and giving them an award, we get them in front of the community with a real marketing push to help them with their mission. The awareness this award creates helps generate donations and volunteers for the non-profits. It also makes it easier for people who need their services to contact them.” Harris said.</p>
<p>The ACLD School &#038; Learning Center was a 2025 WRTA NonProfit Award Winner. </p>
<p>ACLD Executive Director Ricky Fire said “Everyone at ACLD is so grateful. ACLD has received three times the number of school applications we typically get each year. We’ve also worked with several new parents, children and families who found us due to your support,&#8221; Fire said.</p>
<p>Anyone may nominate an organization: a client, a volunteer, a neighbor, a board member, or the nonprofit itself. After a nomination is received, the nonprofit must complete a short response before it can be considered. If someone outside of the organization submits a nomination, the nonprofit must agree to being included in the awards process.</p>
<p> All of the Mahoning Valley’s nonprofits are eligible, including organizations that distribute food, serve youth or veterans, provide health care, support the arts, strengthen neighborhoods and do other community work.</p>
<p>Nominees vie to win an award in one of six categories:</p>
<p>• Community Impact — the organization with a broad footprint or deep measurable service in the community.</p>
<p>• Grassroots Hero — a smaller, neighborhood-focused nonprofit making a large difference with lean resources.</p>
<p>• Innovation in Service— a creative approach, new use of technology, or a new solution to a local challenge.</p>
<p>* Excellence in Collaboration— an organization that partners well with other agencies, volunteers, and local institutions.</p>
<p>• Rising Star— a newer or fast-growing nonprofit that has made a mark in the region.</p>
<p>• Community Cornerstone — an established institution with a long record of service.</p>
<p>The six winners will be announced November 1 so they can attend the Nov 11 awards event. The Nonprofit of the Year will be announced at the November 11 dinner. A representative of each winning organization must attend to receive the award.</p>
<p>Nominations are needed by Tuesday. To nominate a deserving nonprofit go to WRTAonline.com.</p>
]]></content:encoded>

		<title>Civil War soldiers trained in Warren</title>
		<link>https://www.tribtoday.com/news/local-news/2026/10/civil-war-soldiers-trained-in-warren/</link>
		
		<dc:creator><![CDATA[Rick Logan]]></dc:creator>
		<pubDate>Mon, 26 Oct 2026 04:00:59 +0000</pubDate>
				<category><![CDATA[Local News]]></category>
		<guid isPermaLink="false">https://www.tribtoday.com/?p=1492824</guid>

					<description><![CDATA[Did you know there was a Civil War soldier training camp in Warren, Ohio? And who was Henry Harrison Abell? Henry Harrison Abell, born in Bazetta in 1841, enlisted in Company C of the 19th Ohio Infantry in May 1861 with a three-months enlistment. He enlisted again in Company L of the 6th Ohio Cavalry [&#8230;]]]></description>
										<content:encoded><![CDATA[<p>Did you know there was a Civil War soldier training camp in Warren, Ohio? And who was Henry Harrison Abell?</p>
<p>Henry Harrison Abell, born in Bazetta in 1841, enlisted in Company C of the 19th Ohio Infantry in May 1861 with a three-months enlistment. He enlisted again in Company L of the 6th Ohio Cavalry in October and served for the remainder of the year. </p>
<p>The 6th Ohio Volunteer Cavalry was trained at Camp Hutchins in Warren, according to the Trumbull County Historical Society. More than 800 soldiers comprised the cavalry.</p>
<p> Camp Hutchins only remained in use until the end of 1861. During training, the regiment received support from the Warren community in the form of a Thanksgiving feast, a Christmas Eve ball in Warren’s Gaskill House and a New Year’s picnic. </p>
<p>“A beautiful stand of colors was provided by the ‘Mothers and Sisters of the Regiment.’ Its azure canopy was dotted with stars speaking of freedom and featured a full-fledged eagle, bearing in his powerful talons, arrows for the hearts of the invaders and the generous olive branch for yielding foes. </p>
<p>Warren sent off the 6th Cavalry with waving flags, campfires, thronged barracks and soldiers at drill. Crowds gathered to wish the soldiers &#8220;God Speed&#8221; as they departed on their high and dangerous mission.  He was promoted to second lieutenant in December, taking part in battle in the Shenandoah Valley, including Bull Run, Beverly Ford and Five Forks. At Aldie, Virginia, Abell led a cavalry charge against Col. Thomas T. Munford’s Confederate forces and was cited by Gen. Hugh Kilpatrick himself for gallantry. When Gen. Robert E. Lee surrendered, the 6th Ohio Cavalry escorted General Ulysses S. Grant from Appomattox Courthouse to Burkesville Station. The 6th Ohio Cavalry would march one final time in the Grand Review of the Armies in May 1865. </p>
<p>After the war ended, Abell joined the civilian ranks of the 7th Regiment of Cavalry under George A. Custer in 1866. Abell was posted at Fort Morgan and promoted to first lieutenant in 1867. By June, his company was posted at Fort Reynolds, Colorado, where he remained most the year on scouting duty and as adjutant of the company commander, Capt. Gillette. </p>
<p>Abell resigned at the end of 1870 citing a need to handle personal affairs. He died in 1883 and is buried at Oakwood Cemetery in Sharon, Pa.</p>
<p><em>Taylor Galaska is president and marketing director for the Sharon Historical Society.</em></p>
]]></content:encoded>
					
		
		
			</item>

		<title>Nominations sought for WRTA nonprofit awards</title>
		<link>https://www.tribtoday.com/news/local-news/2026/10/nominations-sought-for-wrta-nonprofit-awards/</link>
		
		<dc:creator><![CDATA[Rick Logan]]></dc:creator>
		<pubDate>Mon, 26 Oct 2026 04:00:54 +0000</pubDate>
				<category><![CDATA[Latest News]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[Local News]]></category>
		<guid isPermaLink="false">https://www.tribtoday.com/?p=1492787</guid>

					<description><![CDATA[YOUNGSTOWN —— The Western Reserve Transit Authority is accepting nominations for its 2026 WRTA Nonprofit Awards. Six local nonprofits will be honored at a dinner on Nov. 11 at the B&#038;O Station Banquet Hall, 530 Mahoning Ave., Youngstown. Dean Harris, CEO of WRTA, said each winner receives a WRTA Nonprofit Award, recognition at a special [&#8230;]]]></description>
										<content:encoded><![CDATA[<p>YOUNGSTOWN —— The Western Reserve Transit Authority is accepting nominations for its 2026 WRTA Nonprofit Awards. </p>
<p>Six local nonprofits will be honored at a dinner on Nov. 11 at the B&#038;O Station Banquet Hall, 530 Mahoning Ave., Youngstown.</p>
<p>Dean Harris, CEO of WRTA, said each winner receives a WRTA Nonprofit Award, recognition at a special dinner and a shared regional marketing campaign to help further its mission. One of the six winners will receive an additional honor and be named WRTA 2026 Nonprofit of the Year at the dinner and also receive a $1,000 donation.</p>
<p>Since its inception, 50 Mahoning Valley nonprofits have been recognized. Past nominees and past winners are eligible again after two years.</p>
<p>“Local non-profit organizations do the work most people never see, and the Valley depends on them. Many of their clients, their volunteers, and their staff ride WRTA. So, we try go the extra mile and be a little innovative. Instead of just honoring them at a dinner and giving them an award, we get them in front of the community with a real marketing push to help them with their mission. The awareness this award creates helps generate donations and volunteers for the non-profits. It also makes it easier for people who need their services to contact them.” Harris said.</p>
<p>The ACLD School &#038; Learning Center was a 2025 WRTA NonProfit Award Winner. </p>
<p>ACLD Executive Director Ricky Fire said “Everyone at ACLD is so grateful. ACLD has received three times the number of school applications we typically get each year. We’ve also worked with several new parents, children and families who found us due to your support,&#8221; Fire said.</p>
<p>Anyone may nominate an organization: a client, a volunteer, a neighbor, a board member, or the nonprofit itself. After a nomination is received, the nonprofit must complete a short response before it can be considered. If someone outside of the organization submits a nomination, the nonprofit must agree to being included in the awards process.</p>
<p> All of the Mahoning Valley’s nonprofits are eligible, including organizations that distribute food, serve youth or veterans, provide health care, support the arts, strengthen neighborhoods and do other community work.</p>
<p>Nominees vie to win an award in one of six categories:</p>
<p>• Community Impact — the organization with a broad footprint or deep measurable service in the community.</p>
<p>• Grassroots Hero — a smaller, neighborhood-focused nonprofit making a large difference with lean resources.</p>
<p>• Innovation in Service— a creative approach, new use of technology, or a new solution to a local challenge.</p>
<p>* Excellence in Collaboration— an organization that partners well with other agencies, volunteers, and local institutions.</p>
<p>• Rising Star— a newer or fast-growing nonprofit that has made a mark in the region.</p>
<p>• Community Cornerstone — an established institution with a long record of service.</p>
<p>The six winners will be announced November 1 so they can attend the Nov 11 awards event. The Nonprofit of the Year will be announced at the November 11 dinner. A representative of each winning organization must attend to receive the award.</p>
<p>Nominations are needed by Tuesday. To nominate a deserving nonprofit go to WRTAonline.com.</p>
]]></content:encoded>

		<item>
		<title>Photographer chronicles NFL’s 49ers in Butler exhibition</title>
		<link>https://www.tribtoday.com/news/local-news/2026/10/photographer-chronicles-nfls-49ers-in-butler-exhibition/</link>
		
		<dc:creator><![CDATA[Andy Gray]]></dc:creator>
		<pubDate>Mon, 26 Oct 2026 04:00:50 +0000</pubDate>
				<category><![CDATA[Latest News]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[Local News]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[Local Sports]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[News]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[Ohio Sports]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[Sports]]></category>
		<guid isPermaLink="false">https://www.tribtoday.com/?p=1492469</guid>

					<description><![CDATA[WHAT: “Michael Zagaris: 60 Years of NFL Photography” WHEN: Nov. 1 through April 25, 2027, with an opening reception from 1 to 3 p.m. Nov. 1. Hours are 11 a.m. to 4 p.m. Tuesday through Saturday and noon to 4 p.m. Sunday. WHERE: Butler Institute of American Art, 524 Wick Ave., Youngstown HOW MUCH: Admission [&#8230;]]]></description>
										<content:encoded><![CDATA[<p><strong>WHAT: “Michael Zagaris: 60 Years of NFL Photography”</p>
<p>WHEN: Nov. 1 through April 25, 2027, with an opening reception from 1 to 3 p.m. Nov. 1. Hours are 11 a.m. to 4 p.m. Tuesday through Saturday and noon to 4 p.m. Sunday.</p>
<p>WHERE: Butler Institute of American Art, 524 Wick Ave., Youngstown</p>
<p>HOW MUCH: Admission is free. For more information, go to www.butlerart.com or call 330-743-1107.</strong></p>
<p>Michael Zagaris played football and baseball growing up as a teenager in California and later at George Washington University.</p>
<p>He relied on that experience when he went from playing those sports to photographing them, but it wasn’t the technical knowledge — anticipating what might happen on the field and knowing the best place to stand to capture it — that shaped his work.</p>
<p>“I was shooting action, but I was also doing a lot of stuff on the bench, because I wanted to get up close and personal,” Zagaris said. “I wanted the pictures to convey the feelings I had when I played about what it&#8217;s like to be a player — somebody coming out, sitting on the bench, sweating, in contemplation.”</p>
<p>Now in his 54th year as team photographer for the San Francisco 49ers, Zagaris captured the on-the-field achievements of a team that won five Super Bowl trophies in eight appearances as well as the quieter moments on the sidelines and in the locker room that didn’t make the highlight reels on “SportsCenter.”</p>
<p>“Michael Zagaris: 60 Years of NFL Photography,” which opens Nov. 1 at the Butler Institute of American Art, chronicles that aspect of Zagaris’s colorful, multi-faceted life. </p>
<p><strong>Witness to an assassination</strong></p>
<p>Zagaris, 81, liked photography enough that, as a teenager, he figured out how to duplicate a 49ers photo credential to get on the sidelines and closer to the action, but he never considered it a career. He described himself as an idealistic young man who had his life planned out — a major in politics in college, hopefully a professional football career, followed by runs for the House of Representatives, the Senate and ultimately President.</p>
<p>He was on that path, at least the political part of it. He worked as a page for Pierre Salinger, former press secretary for President John F. Kennedy, when he was appointed to the U.S. Senate representing California following the death of Clair Engle. He later worked for Robert Kennedy when he was a New York Senator.</p>
<p>Zagaris returned to California after graduation and enrolled in law school at Santa Clara University. </p>
<p>“I knew a month into law school, it wasn&#8217;t what I thought it was going to be like,” he said. “I thought, I&#8217;ll finish the year, and then I&#8217;ll see where I am from there.” </p>
<p>When Kennedy announced he was running for President in early 1968, Zagaris worked on the campaign while still pursuing a law degree. He was in Los Angeles on June 5, 1968, for Kennedy’s speech at the Ambassador hotel. </p>
<p>Zagaris was trailing behind the senator shortly after midnight as he was led through the hotel’s kitchen.  </p>
<p>“I’m shuffling along, and as we get to the kitchen, it sounded like somebody let off a string of firecrackers,” he said. “I slipped and almost fell. I’m thinking it’s oil; it’s blood (from) Paul Schrade, the union man that was behind Bobby who had been shot … I still don’t know what’s going on. You can hear yelling and screaming. I remember hearing Ethel’s voice (Kennedy’s wife) and I realize somebody’s been shot and that it was the senator.”</p>
<p>The next morning, Zagaris had to fly back to San Francisco for a contract law final exam, and he heard that Kennedy died after his plane landed. Still numb from the events just hours before, he sat in the classroom and looked at the single-spaced list of questions and the stack of blue books where he was supposed to write his answers.</p>
<p>“I filled eight blue books with how America was f—&#8211; and murdered its leaders,” Zagaris said. “That was the end of law school.” </p>
<p><strong>From aspiring politician to rock photographer</strong></p>
<p>Within two weeks, Zagaris began experimenting with acid after reading Aldous Huxley’s “The Doors of Perception.” He also started hanging out at The Fillmore, a legendary San Francisco concert venue owned by promoter Bill Graham. </p>
<p>“I&#8217;d always been deeply immersed in music in my life, and the bands that were coming through that were really resonating — not just with me, with most people — they were the English bands that were using a lot of our American blues roots that a lot of kids had never heard before,” he said. “They also brought their own style and showmanship, and they were both changing our culture and being changed by it.”</p>
<p>He brought his camera to shows, but Zagaris also chronicled the counterculture, photographing such people as Bobby Seale of the Black Panthers and Harry Edwards, who organized the Olympic Project for Civil Rights, which led to the protests during the 1968 Summer Olympics in Mexico City. It wouldn’t be his last time documenting civil rights activism.</p>
<p>The images that will be shown at the Butler and the ones that fill his website (zmanphotography.com) show incredible technical skill, but Zagaris isn’t someone who rattles off aperture settings and film speeds in discussing his work. He captured his unguarded, behind-the-scenes moments by embedding himself with many of the artists he photographed in the same way a war photographer might embed himself with an army unit. </p>
<p>“Much like an actor that&#8217;s using the Stanislavsky method, where you become what you are, I wanted to do that (with musical acts), and camera was going to be my entry into that world,” Zagaris said. “I would use it as a mirror to document what it&#8217;s like if you are in the band. That was the next best thing to being in the band. The only people that were in those intimate settings were the bands, the road manager, a couple of the roadies, maybe a girlfriend or two, and the dope dealers. I wanted that experience.”</p>
<p>He shot album covers for Peter Frampton, Doobie Brothers, Nicollete Larson and others. He photographed Led Zeppelin’s final U.S. concert and the finale of the Sex Pistols only U.S. tour, which ended with the band breaking up in San Francisco. He captured candid images of classic rock icons such as The Rolling Stones, The Who and Eric Clapton as well as revolutionary artists that followed, including Lou Reed, Patti Smith and The Clash.</p>
<p>“I never made any money to speak of doing that. But I&#8217;m traveling all over, mainly with English bands. You got free travel. You&#8217;re with your guys, and at this point in time, we were all pretty much the same age. Beautiful women, free drugs — all the things that, if you had money, you would have spent it on. And I thought it was one of the great times in my life.”</p>
<p><strong>Back to the gridiron</strong></p>
<p>While still doing music, Zagaris realized he missed photographing sports. He reached out to the public relations director for the 49ers and was told they only give passes to accredited members of the media. Zagaris flipped through the sports publications he regularly read, found the name of the editor of Football Digest, and called again, this time claiming to be that magazine’s editor and asking for access for one of their photographers — Michael Zagaris.</p>
<p>That led to Zagaris shooting the 49ers’ games starting in 1973, and he saw an opportunity to do more when Bill Walsh was hired as head coach in 1979 by the team’s owner, Youngstown native Edward J. DeBartolo Jr.</p>
<p>“I didn’t know Bill, but I knew about him, and I knew he was very cerebral,” Zagaris said. “I got a meeting with him and we hit it off right away. I said, ‘Coach, I think you’re going to do really great things with this organization. I want to document it, not like a sports photographer. I want to shoot this and cover it much like a Presidential photographer covers the White House, which means I have total access, and by that I mean I want to be in the locker room pregame, halftime, post game. I want to be on the bus, on the plane, on the field. I want to cover it totally, not just for the team, but for the league and for the fans. I want to show it in a way that&#8217;s never been shown before, more from the point of view of players and coaches. And I want to be able to take people where they have never been and illuminate what the NFL experience is like.’</p>
<p>“Walsh listened, and he got it.” </p>
<p>Since then, Zagaris has been in right place to capture the Hall of Fame worthy plays of Joe Montana, Jerry Rice, Ronnie Lott and others on the field as well as Montana sitting on the locker room floor at Joe Robbie Stadium at Super Bowl XXIII or a shirtless Eric Wright lying on top of equipment bins in the locker room at halftime. </p>
<p>He also documented the controversy that surrounded quarterback Colin Kaepernick kneeling during the National Anthem in 2016 as a protest against systematic racism and police brutality. The crowd reaction at some stadiums was so intense, Zagaris said he felt as if he was getting a glimpse of what it must have been like for Jackie Robinson in 1947 when he integrated Major League Baseball.</p>
<p>“I thought, ‘This is history. This transcends sports, and I want to cover this.’” Zagaris took the photo of Kaepernick used on the cover of Time magazine.</p>
<p>Zagaris said the opportunities provided by the 49ers are among the many serendipitous moments in his career that also included work for the Oakland Athletics baseball team and Oakland Raiders football team.</p>
<p>“I was very fortunate that I was where I was, and that he (Walsh) was the person they brought in as head coach,” Zagaris said. “I&#8217;ve been with an organization, the 49ers, first with the DeBartolo family and then with the (Denise DeBartolo) York family, that&#8217;s allowed me the freedom of expression to document this.</p>
<p>“I don&#8217;t believe in coincidences. I believe everything happens the way it&#8217;s supposed to. I&#8217;ve been incredibly lucky to have all these things and all these people intersect in my life.”</p>
]]></content:encoded>

		<title>Joy of reading takes flight at Howland aviation museum</title>
		<link>https://www.tribtoday.com/news/local-news/2026/10/joy-of-reading-takes-flight-at-howland-aviation-museum/</link>
		
		<dc:creator><![CDATA[Rick Logan]]></dc:creator>
		<pubDate>Mon, 26 Oct 2026 04:00:47 +0000</pubDate>
				<category><![CDATA[Local News]]></category>
		<guid isPermaLink="false">https://www.tribtoday.com/?p=1492810</guid>

					<description><![CDATA[HOWLAND — As part of the month of October’s Trumbull County Reads celebration with the theme of flight, Warren Trumbull County Public Library hosted Library Day at Ernie Hall Aviation Museum on Saturday. All area libraries read the book, &#8220;To Slip the Bonds of Earth,” by Akron writer Amanda Flowers. The main character in the [&#8230;]]]></description>
										<content:encoded><![CDATA[<p>HOWLAND — As part of the month of October’s Trumbull County Reads celebration with the theme of flight, Warren Trumbull County Public Library hosted Library Day at Ernie Hall Aviation Museum on Saturday.</p>
<p>    All area libraries read the book, &#8220;To Slip the Bonds of Earth,” by Akron writer Amanda Flowers. The main character in the book is Katherine Wright, the sister of Orville and Wilbur Wright. The setting is in Dayton where the family lived. The time period is when the brothers were working in Kitty Hawk, North Carolina, where they achieved flight.</p>
<p>  “This was an opportunity for people to visit the museum  who may not have been here before and for the library to assist with crafts and the bookmobile. This fit perfectly with our theme,” said Cheryl Bush, marketing and public relations manager at Warren Trumbull County Public Library. If guests showed their Trumbull County library card entrance to the museum was half price.</p>
<p>      The site worked with the library&#8217;s premise. The man for whom the museum was named, Ernie Craft Hall, met the Wright Brothers.</p>
<p>     “ Hall was born in 1890 in Gustavus, Ohio. His family moved to the Packard House in Warren. He was interested in flight, and he built gliders while living there,” said museum tour guide Bob Griffin of Warren.</p>
<p>      Hall purchased a kit for the French plane, Bleriot and flew it on August 30, 1911.</p>
<p>      Through newspaper articles, he became aware of the Wright brothers and their successful flights. So, Hall went to Dayton to meet them.</p>
<p>    “He befriended them and purchased their plane, the Model G Seaplane. He then began a pilot school with that plane in Conneaut, Pennsylvania,&#8221;  said Griffin. </p>
<p>    The federal government hired Hall to train military pilots in Texas for  World War I flying missions.</p>
<p>    Upon his return to the Mahoning Valley, he purchased land near Howland Township and continued training pilots.</p>
<p>     Griffin said, ”The Wright Brothers inspired him. Hall received patents for landing gear and aviation devices.”  The Wright Brothers were known for acquiring patents to protect their inventions.</p>
<p>     The five members of the  Witt family of Warren visited the museum on Library Day.</p>
<p>    “The boys (her sons, Jayden,11 and Kasen, 9) are very interested in flying,” said their mother, Kristina Witt.   </p>
<p>     Her three children, including her daughter, attended WTCPL’s Homeschool Detectives’ Paper Airplane Science workshop last week.</p>
<p>  WTCPL Community Engagement Associate Trish Fatoben and Lori Faust, community engagement manager, greeted museum guests at the bookmobile on the front lawn offering the opportunity to sign up for a library card or check out a book.</p>
<p>     This was an additional chance for Howland residents to interact locally with the library while their branch is renovated. It is closed until next spring.  </p>
<p>     Warren Library Friends offered crafts and book marker giveaways while Adult Services Manager Alyse McMullen and Adult Services Associate Madison Diefenderfer provided flight- themed activities.</p>
<p> Witt, who homeschools her children after moving here from Missouri three years ago, said, “I am always looking for educational opportunities for the children.”</p>
<p>Griffin not only provided information about the refurbished and replica airplanes in the museum, but he also gave the children the opportunity to sit in the planes and experience what it was like to travel in a vintage plane.</p>
<p> One plane on display was a 1947 Piper 12 that was Hall’s personal travel plane.</p>
<p> Library  Day provided information to local residents about the importance of Ohio residents to the advancement of flight and to know that some of those people came from Trumbull County.</p>
]]></content:encoded>

										<content:encoded><![CDATA[<p>Champion and Warren township officials plan a public meeting Nov. 4 to discuss what needs to be on the books to handle a data center if one were to locate in either township.</p>
<p>Champion Trustee Chairman Doug Emerine said to date, the Champion zoning department has not been approached by anyone about a data center. He said the township zoning board is working on guidelines and has been reviewing what Hubbard Township and other communities have done.</p>
<p>Emerine said one property that has been discussed is the former Copperweld Steel property off state Route 45 that straddles both townships. He said the 360-acre site has one-third of the land in Champion and two-thirds in Warren Township.</p>
<p>Emerine said of the available properties in Champion, that one would be the one to locate a data center on, and the zoning department needs to be prepared.</p>
<p>&#8220;Right now we need to get as much information as we can on data centers. I am not sure what Warren Township officials want to do, but we need to get together and discuss this,&#8221; Emerine said.</p>
<p>He said if a data center or any other large business were to express interest in locating in Champion, there would be public meetings to keep residents informed.</p>
<p>&#8220;No matter what, there will be a data center located somewhere in Trumbull County,&#8221; Emerine said.</p>
<p>Warren Township Trustee Ed Anthony said he agrees with Emerine that the Copperweld property would be the one where a data center would possibly locate.</p>
<p>&#8220;It is prime property because of the available land and being by the river, which would provide water, but we have not received any inquiries,&#8221; Anthony said.</p>
<p>He said the property does have many cleanup issues and there is litigation still on the property.</p>
<p>Anthony said the township&#8217;s zoning board has been reviewing what to include in the zoning codes.</p>
<p>Anthony said a joint meeting is being planned for 6 p.m. Nov. 4 at the Champion administration building.</p>
<p>Also, Champion officials approved providing between $6,000 and $7,000 for the road department to do drainage upgrade work this year on Marshall Road.</p>
<p>Emerine said the money will cover the costs for materials and supplies needed for the work.</p>
<p>In another matter, Vanessa Teuscher found a bulletin and a service schedule from Dec. 1, 1985, when the township cemetery chapel was dedicated. She had the two items framed and they will be placed inside the chapel.</p>

		<item>
		<title>Hubbard Army veteran fought, taught over 3 decades</title>
		<link>https://www.tribtoday.com/news/local-news/2026/10/hubbard-army-veteran-fought-taught-over-3-decades/</link>
		
		<dc:creator><![CDATA[Rick Logan]]></dc:creator>
		<pubDate>Mon, 26 Oct 2026 04:00:36 +0000</pubDate>
				<category><![CDATA[Local News]]></category>
		<guid isPermaLink="false">https://www.tribtoday.com/?p=1492790</guid>

					<description><![CDATA[For more than three decades, Brent Herzberger answered the call to serve, building a military career that took him from the deserts of Kuwait and Iraq to leadership positions training soldiers and supporting military operations across Ohio. Along the way, the Brookfield High School graduate gained lifelong friendships, valuable lessons and a deep appreciation for [&#8230;]]]></description>
										<content:encoded><![CDATA[<p>For more than three decades, Brent Herzberger answered the call to serve, building a military career that took him from the deserts of Kuwait and Iraq to leadership positions training soldiers and supporting military operations across Ohio. </p>
<p>Along the way, the Brookfield High School graduate gained lifelong friendships, valuable lessons and a deep appreciation for those who have worn the nation&#8217;s uniform.</p>
<p>Born in Sharon, Pa., and raised in Masury, Herzberger graduated from Brookfield High School in 1989. The oldest of three children and the only son, he spent much of his youth outdoors, participating in Boy Scouts and exploring the woods with friends.</p>
<p>“I spent a lot of summers playing Army,” Herzberger recalled. “As I entered high school, my mind was definitely focused toward military service.”</p>
<p>That desire to serve was shaped by family members who served in previous wars. His grandfathers were World War II veterans, and an uncle retired from the U.S. Air Force. Listening to their stories instilled a strong sense of patriotism and duty.</p>
<p>After moving to Florida following graduation, Herzberger voluntarily enlisted in the Army in December 1989.</p>
<p>His military career began as a combat engineer and ribbon bridge crew member before transitioning into construction engineering specialties. As he advanced through the ranks, his responsibilities grew. He helped establish three new engineer units under the 112th Engineer Battalion at Camp James A. Garfield and later served as a Regional Training Institute course manager, overseeing leadership training for newly promoted sergeants. His final assignment was as a Range Operations noncommissioned officer at Camp James A. Garfield, where he managed personnel and training operations at the 21,000-acre military training center.</p>
<p>Like many veterans, he remembers basic training as an adjustment.</p>
<p>“It was a culture shock going from your schedule to their schedule,” he said. “But eventually you got into a rhythm.”</p>
<p>His first deployment came during Operation Desert Storm in 1990. While there were long periods of waiting before the ground war began, life quickly changed once operations started.</p>
<p>“We were living out of our vehicles, always on the move,” Herzberger said. “No tents, latrines or mess halls.”</p>
<p>His second deployment, with Charlie Company, 216th Engineer Battalion of Austintown during Operation Iraqi Freedom in 2004, was markedly different. Engineers conducted hundreds of combat patrols, logged thousands of equipment hours and completed major force-protection projects to support troops throughout Iraq.</p>
<p>One of the lasting effects of deployment, he said, is the heightened awareness many veterans carry home with them.</p>
<p>“You find yourself scanning the road when traveling, identifying exits when you enter a room and observing everything and everyone coming into your space,” he said. “Stay alert to stay alive.”</p>
<p>Among his most vivid memories are the soldiers his battalion lost during the 2004-05 deployment.</p>
<p>“They will never be forgotten,” he said.</p>
<p>Despite the hardships, Herzberger says the friendships formed during military service remain one of the greatest rewards of his career.</p>
<p>“These are some of the best people I&#8217;ve had the honor of serving with,” he said. “The military family is real.”</p>
<p>Looking back, he credits the Army with giving him discipline, purpose and direction. During his years of service, he earned a bachelor&#8217;s degree and graduated summa cum laude. He also credits the military mindset with helping him raise successful children and achieve personal and professional goals.</p>
<p>After serving 33½ years — 10 in the National Guard and the remainder on active duty — Herzberger retired from the Army in June 2023. Military service continues to be a family tradition; his son is currently serving in the Air Force.</p>
<p>Today, he remains active in the veteran community as senior vice commander of Hubbard VFW Post 3767, helping fellow veterans connect with resources and maintain the bonds forged through military service.</p>
<p>Retirement has also allowed him more time with family and a new passion: participating in a World War II living history and reenactment group.</p>
<p>“We can&#8217;t let that piece of history fade away,” he said. “We must become the stewards of their stories and keep their service and sacrifices alive.”</p>
<p>For Herzberger, preserving the legacy of those who served before him is simply another way of continuing a lifetime of service.</p>
]]></content:encoded>
					
		
		
			</item>

					<description><![CDATA[WARREN — Twenty of the most impressive young leaders in Trumbull County have been selected for the 2026 Class of Twenty Under 20. Now in its 14th year, the program is co-sponsored by the Tribune Chronicle and AVI Foodsystems. Twenty Under 20 honors 20 high-achieving individuals younger than 20 for their leadership skills through volunteerism, [&#8230;]]]></description>
										<content:encoded><![CDATA[<p>WARREN — Twenty of the most impressive young leaders in Trumbull County have been selected for the 2026 Class of Twenty Under 20.</p>
<p>Now in its 14th year, the program is co-sponsored by the Tribune Chronicle and AVI Foodsystems.</p>
<p>Twenty Under 20 honors 20 high-achieving individuals younger than 20 for their leadership skills through volunteerism, extracurricular activities and academic achievements.</p>
<p>Sue Shafer, community events coordinator for the Tribune Chronicle, said it was difficult for the judges to choose just 20 winners out of more than 60 nominations, which included six repeat nominees.</p>
<p>“There are so many outstanding students this year,” Shafer said. “This group has impacted the community in significant ways, not just at their school.” </p>
<p>She also said there was a wide variety of school districts represented, with 11. </p>
<p>The students, whose volunteer activities run the gamut from church groups to Akron Children’s Hospital and creating new projects such as a food pantry, were nominated by family members, teachers, club advisers and school guidance counselors.</p>
<p>Many of them have 4.0 grade-point averages, have taken college credit courses and are on track to graduate at the top of their classes.</p>
<p>The winners were selected based on a calculation of 50% leadership and 25% each academic achievement and extracurricular activities.</p>
<p>The top five winners will be selected to split $1,000 to be donated to the local charity of their choice. One overall winner will be picked from among the five. Those winners will be announced during an awards dinner Dec. 9.</p>
<p>This year’s class includes:</p>
<p>• Samantha Barkley, Warren G. Harding senior</p>
<p>• Payton Borsic, Warren G. Harding senior</p>
<p>• Aliah Brumit, Maplewood senior</p>
<p>• Micaiah Capers, Warren G. Harding senior</p>
<p>• Jacob Clisby, Bristol senior</p>
<p>• Dylan Davis, Lordstown senior</p>
<p>• Peyton Evans, Newton Falls junior</p>
<p>• Jane Faulk, Warren G. Harding senior</p>
<p>• Trudi Herrholtz, Lakeview senior</p>
<p>• Minhee Hong, Girard senior</p>
<p>• Ace Johnson, Hubbard senior</p>
<p>• Noah Lane, Newton Falls senior</p>
<p>• Gianna Magazine, Warren G. Harding senior</p>
<p>• Alaina McFarland, Girard senior</p>
<p>• Grayson Meszaros, Girard senior</p>
<p>• Sanaa Pitts, Warren G. Harding junior</p>
<p>• Mae Robinson, Champion senior</p>
<p>• Brady Snelson, Howland senior</p>
<p>• Harlow Stone, Niles McKinley junior</p>
<p>• Alaina Wagner, Warren G. Harding senior</p>
<p>Tickets for the Dec. 9 awards dinner are available by contacting Shafer at sshafer@tribtoday.com and 330-841-1696.</p>

		<item>
		<title>Obama lambastes Trump in NE Ohio rally for Acton, Brown</title>
		<link>https://www.tribtoday.com/news/local-news/2026/10/obama-lambastes-trump-in-ne-ohio-rally-for-acton-brown/</link>
		
		<dc:creator><![CDATA[Rick Logan]]></dc:creator>
		<pubDate>Mon, 26 Oct 2026 04:00:15 +0000</pubDate>
				<category><![CDATA[Local News]]></category>
		<guid isPermaLink="false">https://www.tribtoday.com/?p=1492708</guid>

					<description><![CDATA[CLEVELAND — Former President Barack Obama seized on what he described as cruelty and indecency coming from President Donald Trump and his Republican allies, stepping into one of the nation’s ugliest midterm fights as he worked to rally Democratic voters in battleground Ohio on Saturday. Obama took specific aim at Republican gubernatorial candidate Vivek Ramaswamy, [&#8230;]]]></description>
										<content:encoded><![CDATA[
<p>CLEVELAND — Former President Barack Obama seized on what he described as cruelty and indecency coming from President Donald Trump and his Republican allies, stepping into one of the nation’s ugliest midterm fights as he worked to rally Democratic voters in battleground Ohio on Saturday.</p>
<p>Obama took specific aim at Republican gubernatorial candidate Vivek Ramaswamy, who has doubled down on an advertising campaign featuring the estranged mother of Democratic rival Dr. Amy Acton. Trump and Vice President JD Vance have also defended the ad, even as Republican leaders across Ohio, including Gov. Mike DeWine, have called for its removal.</p>
<p>&#8220;In recent days, we&#8217;ve seen there is no line he will not cross, no low he will not sink to,&#8221; Obama said of Ramaswamy, noting that Acton has &#8220;spoken movingly about her painful childhood and all she&#8217;s experienced: abuse, neglect and homelessness.&#8221;</p>
<p>&#8220;There&#8217;s a decency we should expect out of our public officials,&#8221; Obama continued. &#8220;And I don&#8217;t know what&#8217;s happened where the standards of behavior are so low that somebody could run an ad like that.&#8221;</p>
<p>Obama, 65, led the rally at Cleveland Public Auditorium before an estimated crowd of about 10,000.</p>
<p> He remains the Democratic Party&#8217;s best motivator nearly a decade after leaving the White House. He later campaigned Saturday night in Michigan for Democrats in that state</p>
<p>In Cleveland, Obama shared the stage with Acton and former three-term Ohio Sen. Sherrod Brown, who is looking to unseat Republican Sen. Jon Husted.</p>
<p>Although Ohio has become more reliably Republican, it’s been a hotly contested battleground this year. Trump and Vance have both visited the state in recent weeks to drum up support for Husted and Ramaswamy. And the controversy over Ramaswamy’s ad has ratcheted up the already high temperature.</p>
<p>Ramaswamy has defended his decision to press ahead with the ad as fair game. Trump aggressively defended the ad, which has emerged as a national flashpoint in the midterms’ final days, as he voted in West Palm Beach on Saturday.</p>
<p>&#8220;This is a disgrace,” Trump said. &#8220;The mother said that Amy Acton is a liar and she&#8217;s no good. And you know what? I don&#8217;t think you can get a better reference on that.&#8221; He added that if his own mother had said anything bad about him, it would be front and center.</p>
<p>The Ramaswamy campaign video features Acton&#8217;s birth mother, Donna Arthur, calling her &#8220;a liar&#8221; and insinuating she has mental health issues.</p>
<p>Acton, Ohio&#8217;s COVID-19-era health director, has made no secret of facing homelessness and abuse as a child. Arthur lost custody of her children after their stepfather, Arthur&#8217;s husband, was indicted on a charge of rape involving Acton.</p>
<p>The abuse allegedly happened from December 1976 to April 1978. Cledith Arthur failed to show for a court appearance and became a fugitive. Donna Arthur remained married to him until his death in 2004.</p>
<p>Appearing before Obama, Acton spoke as if addressing Ramaswamy directly.</p>
<p>&#8220;My opponent has chosen to exploit, intentionally, my childhood to save his stumbling campaign, but it&#8217;s not going to work,” she said. &#8220;Decency still matters.&#8221; She added: &#8220;Mr. Ramaswamy, you are not just out of touch, you are out for yourself and you are a danger to my state. Apparently there is no low to which you won&#8217;t stoop.&#8221;</p>
<p>After wrapping up in Ohio, Obama led a rally in Detroit for Abdul El-Sayed, a former public health official who is working to unite the Democratic base in Michigan after winning a divisive primary by less than a percentage point. El-Sayed is running against former Rep. Mike Rogers, a Republican.</p>
<p>Obama&#8217;s appearance in the city, which is three-quarters Black, could bolster support for El-Sayed among Black voters. In the primary, his opponent Haley Stevens won Wayne County, which includes Detroit, by about 6 percentage points. Former Vice President Kamala Harris, the first black and South Asian vice president, campaigned for El-Sayed in September.</p>
<p>In Ohio, Obama also called out Trump’s inability to lower costs, a key campaign promise, noting that the president has instead focused on building a new White House ballroom and helipad on the South Lawn.</p>
<p>&#8220;I don&#8217;t know if you&#8217;ve noticed, but he seems very focused on home renovation,” he quipped. &#8220;He should be on HGTV.&#8221; He went on: &#8220;There’s gold everywhere. He&#8217;s got the White House looking like Saddam Hussein opened a Cheesecake Factory.&#8221;</p>
<p>The former Democratic president acknowledged that the nation has Trump for another two years, until his term ends. But voters are not stuck with Trump&#8217;s allies, he said.</p>
<p>&#8220;We&#8217;re not stuck with a spineless Congress and Republican governors who are handling lawlessness and carelessness and cruelty and indifference for working people,&#8221; Obama said. &#8220;After two years of chaos and cruelty and corruption and incompetence &#8230; you get a chance to say enough is enough.&#8221;</p>
<p>Brown urged the crowd to seek change in their Senate leadership.</p>
<p>“Ohioans won&#8217;t stand for this rigged system any longer,” Brown said. “Ohio&#8217;s fighting back.”</p>
<p>The Husted campaign released a statement after Brown’s appearance with Obama.</p>
<p>“The sight of Sherrod Brown and Barack Obama together again should send shivers down Ohioans’ spines,” it read. “The Brown-Obama duo gave us higher taxes, less jobs and wide-open borders. Ohioans are ready to retire Sherrod Brown once and for all by electing Jon Husted on Nov. 3.”</p>
]]></content:encoded>

	<item>
		<title>Dolores (Montgomery) Linnon</title>
		<link>https://butlerradio.com/dolores-montgomery-linnon/</link>
		
		<dc:creator><![CDATA[Pat Parker]]></dc:creator>
		<pubDate>Sun, 25 Oct 2026 21:03:36 +0000</pubDate>
				<category><![CDATA[Obits]]></category>
		<guid isPermaLink="false">https://butlerradio.com/?p=34340282</guid>

					<description><![CDATA[<p>Dolores (Montgomery) Linnon, 95, of Butler, Pennsylvania, passed away on Friday, October 23, 2026, in Butler. In a poignant coincidence, she passed away on the same date as her beloved husband, John, who preceded her in death exactly 24 years earlier. Born on December 26, 1930, in Brackenridge, Pennsylvania, Dolores was the daughter of the [&#8230;]</p>
<p>The post <a href="https://butlerradio.com/dolores-montgomery-linnon/">Dolores (Montgomery) Linnon</a> appeared first on <a href="https://butlerradio.com">ButlerRadio.com - Butler, PA</a>.</p>
]]></description>
										<content:encoded><![CDATA[<p>Dolores (Montgomery) Linnon, 95, of Butler, Pennsylvania, passed away on Friday, October 23, 2026, in Butler. In a poignant coincidence, she passed away on the same date as her beloved husband, John, who preceded her in death exactly 24 years earlier.</p>
<p>Born on December 26, 1930, in Brackenridge, Pennsylvania, Dolores was the daughter of the late Chester Elwood Montgomery and Dolores Arnold Montgomery. She was the oldest of three sisters.</p>
<p>A 1948 graduate of Har-Brack High School, Dolores went on to work as an executive secretary at Alcoa. She had a love of learning and pursued numerous college courses in business and nursing.</p>
<p>On October 20, 1956, she married John Linnon, with whom she shared 46 happy years of marriage before his passing in 2002.</p>
<p>One of her greatest joys in life was being a mother. She poured her heart into raising her four sons, creating a home filled with love, laughter, and a warm welcome for all. Her door was always open, and many neighborhood children gathered around her table, enjoying a home-cooked meal. As her family grew, she embraced being a grandmother with that same boundless love and devotion, taking pride and joy in her grandchildren. For Dolores, family was always at the heart of everything she did.</p>
<p>Dolores was a woman of deep Christian faith who loved the Lord and found great joy in reading and sharing the Gospels. Her faith was not simply something she spoke about, but something she lived through her kindness, compassion, and genuine concern for others.</p>
<p>Throughout her life, Dolores was actively involved in her community, including the Butler County Symphony, the American Cancer Society, and Meals on Wheels.</p>
<p>Those who knew Dolores will remember her remarkable optimism and joyful outlook on life. Even in the most difficult circumstances, she remained positive and lifted the spirits of those around her. She possessed a wonderful sense of humor and a gift for storytelling, often sharing memories with a familiar twinkle in her eye.</p>
<p>Blessed with an exceptionally sharp memory, Dolores could recall names, dates, and family stories with accuracy, even in her final days. She had a passion for genealogy and took great pleasure in preserving and sharing her family’s history. She was also known for her keen intuition.</p>
<p>Above all, Dolores will be remembered as a genuinely kind and caring woman whose faith, laughter, wisdom, and unwavering positivity were gifts to everyone fortunate enough to know her.</p>
<p>She is survived by her four sons, Michael Linnon and Timothy Linnon, both of Butler; Patrick (Therese) Linnon of Hermitage; and Brion (Erin) Linnon of Butler; her beloved grandchildren, Connor and Madeleine Linnon; her sister, Jane (Al) Bittcher of Zelienople; and numerous nieces and nephews.</p>
<p>In addition to her parents and husband, Dolores was preceded in death by her grandson, Ethan Linnon, and her sister, Shirley (Ray) Schmuhl.</p>
<p>Dolores leaves behind a legacy of faith, love, kindness, and joy. While she will be deeply missed, her family takes comfort in the countless memories she created the wisdom she shared, and the example she set of finding goodness and happiness in every season of life. Her beautiful spirit and the twinkle in her eye will never be forgotten.</p>
<p>Family and friends will be received on Tuesday, October 27, 2026, from 5-7 PM at SPENCER D. GEIBEL FUNERAL HOME &amp; CREMATION SERVICES, 140 NEW CASTLE RD. BUTLER, PA 16001. <a href="http://www.spencerdgeibel.com/" target="_blank" rel="noopener" data-saferedirecturl="https://www.google.com/url?q=http://WWW.SPENCERDGEIBEL.COM&amp;source=gmail&amp;ust=1793048226513000&amp;usg=AOvVaw0cbfiHRt6tDxdEV-zZ7g3K">0WWW.SPENCERDGEIBEL.COM</a>. A prayer service will immediately follow in the funeral home. Burial will be held privately.</p>

<p>Red Ribbon Week begins Monday and runs through Friday in the Mars Area School District.</p>
<p>Students at Mars Area schools will be wearing pajamas tomorrow with the theme of “Your Choices are the Key to Your Dreams”.</p>
<p>Other theme days include wearing tie dye on Tuesday, Be The Kind Kid clothing Wednesday, Halloween colors on Thursday, and workout or sweat clothes on Friday.</p>
<p>Red Ribbon Week is an annual drug and alcohol prevention awareness campaign sponsored by the National Family Partnership.</p>
<p>The Mars Area Centennial School is also collecting donations of belts and decorative pillows for a STEAM-inspired library project.</p>

										<content:encoded><![CDATA[<p>Butler County Community College is celebrating the success of their annual golf outing, which once again raised six figures.</p>
<p>The 31<sup>st</sup> annual BC3 Education Foundation golf outing was held earlier this month at Olde Stonewall Golf Club and raised $109,000.</p>
<p>Among 30 teams and 120 participants, the team from First National Bank finished with a 16 under 54 in the scramble format to achieve three consecutive titles.</p>
<p>Since the golfing outing’s inaugural year in 1996—the annual fundraiser has raised nearly $1.7 million. The proceeds are used to fund adult literacy, addiction recovery, financial programs, food security and other BC3 initiatives.</p>

										<content:encoded><![CDATA[<p>Lee Croll, 85, of Slippery Rock passed away on Sunday, October 17, 2026. He was born in West Liberty on July 21, 1941, to the late Delbert and Grace (Double) Croll. Lee graduated from Slippery Rock High School, and then honorably served in the U.S. Army.</p>
<p>He worked at Kennametal, Zedmark and Minerals Technologies doing industrial maintenance. Lee was known for being strong willed and was very handy at expertly fixing things. He enjoyed hunting and fishing. Lee was a long-time member of West Liberty United Methodist Church.</p>
<p>Lee was the beloved husband of the late Kay Morrow, whom he married on September 10, 1965; father of Roger (Beth), Dean and Carrie Croll; brother of Frances Lewis; grandfather of Cody (Kailee) Croll and Wyatt (Isabella McNelis) Croll; great grandfather of Karson Croll.</p>
<p>Along with his parents and wife, Lee was preceded in death by his siblings: Delbert, Elmer, Samuel, Harry and David Croll, Hanna Hartman, Margaret McConnell and Ethel Maxwell; and his half-brothers: Jim, Barney and Walker Croll and Charles Wolford.</p>
<p>All services were held privately through SPENCER D. GEIBEL FUNERAL HOME &amp; CREMATION SERVICES, 140 NEW CASTLE RD. BUTLER, PA 16001. <a href="http://www.spencerdgeibel.com/" target="_blank" rel="noopener" data-saferedirecturl="https://www.google.com/url?q=http://WWW.SPENCERDGEIBEL.COM&amp;source=gmail&amp;ust=1792852185797000&amp;usg=AOvVaw3ZsFVu5zKoZE4DJfLKutMm">WWW.SPENCERDGEIBEL.COM</a>. In lieu of flowers, Lee would like everyone to make sure they paint their hammers red and go buy themselves breakfast at his favorite restaurant, “The Camelot”.</p>
<p>The post <a href="https://butlerradio.com/lee-croll/">Lee Croll</a> appeared first on <a href="https://butlerradio.com">ButlerRadio.com - Butler, PA</a>.</p>
